# The harness for the third engine: design

This is the design of record for the harness. It is one living document: every section is at its current value, and the ledger in §1 records each revision with what changed, why, and the check that would show it wrong. Git holds the dated versions. `research/harness-research.r1.md` and `research/harness-research.r2.md` are the two earlier revisions, kept unedited as records; they are not read for anything this file marks current, and the "highest `N` wins" naming rule no longer applies to this note. Design of record for the engine: `third-engine-design.md`, one living document with a ledger on top, with `jit-design.md` for the compiled tier in the same shape; the `rN` files are records and are not read for anything current. Vocabulary: `CONTEXT.md`. Decisions: `docs/adr/0001` to `0006`. Architecture terms (module, interface, seam, adapter, depth, leverage, locality) are the `codebase-design` vocabulary.

**Question.** What should a harness, in Böckeler's sense, look like for the development and maintenance of the third engine, and how much of it does the design already prescribe? And, from revision 3 on: what shape does the harness itself have to be so that it does not drift the way the design revisions have already begun to?

**Sources.** The full citation lists are in `research/harness-research.r1.md` ("Sources") and `research/harness-research.r2.md` ("Sources"); this file cites them by the same short names (**HE**, **CE**, **Sensors**, **OpenAI**, **Stripe**, **Anthropic**, **Approved**, **CM** for `computed`, **FH-dts**, **FH-issue**, **Hooks-ref**, **Plugins-ref**). Revision 3 adds one input: the architecture review of 2026-09-07 over both earlier revisions and the design of record, with a citation inventory of every harness prescription in `research/third-engine-design.r1.md` to `.r7.md`, `research/jit-design.r3.md`, `research/llvm-research.r1.md` and the ADRs.

**7. Revision 7 (2026-09-08).** Two readings of §5 changed after `done` (#6) and `new-ext` (#9) landed. The map's ceiling is 150 lines, not 100: the rewritten map (`974a41f`) carries the four commitments and the six regions, and at 100 lines the regions alone left no room for the prose the map test names. And `vocab` no longer reads a lock: the lines of `api.lock` mirror php's own names (`sapi_name`, `set_exception_handler`, `unregister_tick_function`), which the engine copies on purpose, so the assertion could only fail on the names php chose. Public item names and rustdoc prose are still read. Check: `cargo test -p xtask --test map` holds the map at 150; `cargo xtask vocab` passes on a `php_ext` whose lock names php's API.

---

## 0. The one-paragraph design

**One manifest, one reading, one comparison, one judge, one status.** The rules of the harness live in one tracked data file, `seams.toml`, and every rule sensor is derived from it. Every instrument ends by writing one reading of one shape to `target/harness/`, and everything an agent or a person reads about the state of the engine is a rendering of those readings by `xtask status`. Every comparison against the oracle crosses one seam in the oracle module, which owns the one table of accepted differences. The edit-time gate is one instrument, `xtask permit`, and both kinds of Claude Code hook are thin adapters over it; the definition of done is one instrument, `xtask done`, called by the Stop hook, by pre-push and by CI. The harness note is one living document, and it asks the design of record to become one too.

---

## 1. Ledger: what changed, and why

Each entry is numbered as the revision that made it and names the check that would show it wrong.

**1. Revision 1 (2026-09-05).** Read HE and its neighbours; found that the design never says "harness" but that nineteen of its mechanisms are guides or sensors, almost all computational, the guides structural (types, crate visibility, locks); found no agent map, no local wiring of sensors into the edit loop, and no rule for repeated agent failures. Proposed a four-layer harness (context, constraint, feedback, maintenance) in the crate map, with every check an `xtask` subcommand and hooks as thin wires. Check: §3's table maps to the design's own sections; a mechanism it lists that the design does not have is a mistake in the table.

**2. Revision 2 (2026-09-05).** Read `computed` and Function Hooks from primary sources. Made the map's facts computed regions, added a gitignored local region file, moved the session ritual into a `prompt.context` function hook, folded the two shell hooks into one `tool.call` function hook with the rules in TypeScript, added steering-loop capture, and listed the fail-open hazard as the largest new gap. Check: a gitignored region file renders when named (verified by a scratch run recorded in `research/harness-research.r2.md` A2.1); the installed binary exposes the events used (verified against the declaration file it writes).

**3. Revision 3 (2026-09-07).** An architecture review found the sensors deep and everything around them shallow. The rules the sensors enforce were prose in five design revisions and re-typed by hand into `xtask`, a clippy configuration, TypeScript regexes and the agent map; the inventory found the lock set enumerated four incompatible ways, two `unsafe` allowance lists in force at once, and the oracle pin stated as 8.5.9 in one file and 8.5.7 in another, before any code exists. Sensor results reached the agent through nine separately wired channels. "We differ from the oracle on purpose" was three tables of three shapes. "Done" was six conditions of prose and a one-condition file protocol. And the harness note itself needed both revisions read in full, the disease it diagnosed in the design. Six changes:

1. **The seam manifest** (§4.1). One tracked data file declares each seam once: producer, consumers, lock file, permitted edges, `unsafe` homes, forbidden types with reasons, line ceilings, the ADR that decided it, the sentence a failure prints. `api-check`, `unsafe-audit`, `budget`, the generated `ext/clippy.toml`, `permit`, the map's region and one negative-control fixture per rule are all derived from it. The design revision cites the manifest instead of restating numbers.
2. **The reading** (§4.2). Every instrument writes one value of one shape; regions, the done gate, the steering ledger and CI telemetry are renderers over the store, not stores of their own.
3. **`xtask permit`** (§4.4). The edit-time rules move out of TypeScript into one instrument that judges an intended tool call. The shipped command hook and the optional function hook are both adapters over it, so the seam has two real adapters and fail-open is covered. `rules.ts`, `tsconfig.json` and the generated declaration file leave the harness unless the function hook is enabled.
4. **One table of divergences** (§4.3). The oracle module gains the comparison and owns the one table of accepted differences; witnesses, phpt verdicts, the runner control, compile identity and trace dumps all consult it by a stable id. The JIT note's "recorded differences table" and revision 4's "named list of known disagreements" are rows in it.
5. **`xtask done`** (§4.5). The definition of done is one instrument computed from git and the readings, called by the Stop hook, pre-push and CI. The per-turn file protocol between a function hook and a shell script is gone.
6. **A living document** (§10 rule 7, §14). This note is one file at its current state with this ledger on top. It asks the design of record to take the same shape.

Check: the six are wrong if the manifest cannot express a rule the design has (§4.1 lists every rule it must carry; one that does not fit is the counterexample), if a reading of one shape cannot carry what a region needs (§5's regions are each one `status` call; a region that needs a second command is the counterexample), or if `permit` is too slow at the tool call (§4.4 gives the bar and the fallback).

**4. Revision 4 (2026-09-07).** The review that produced design ledger 8 and JIT ledger 4 found the manifest's example disagreeing with the design it exists to carry: three crate budgets that were not the design's, the `unsafe` homes declared twice (once per `[[crate]]` row and once as a rule), and a `php_bundle` edge that omitted `php_ext`, on which the bundle depends for `ExtensionBuilder` and the `Nop` alias. It also found no lane for the tier's own sensors. Four changes: the example manifest carries the design's numbers and declares `unsafe` once (§4.1); the `php_bundle` edge names `php_ext`; §7 gains the tier's lanes, `force` and `exit-fuzz` per commit on the witnesses and nightly on `Zend/tests` and `tests/`, the trace-dump diff under `inline`, and `port-map` as an instrument with a reading; §14 records which of its asks the design of record took in its ledger 8 (items 1 in part, 2, 3, 5 and 6) and what remains. Check: `xtask budget` against the manifest passes with the design's §4.1 numbers and no others; a second statement of the `unsafe` homes anywhere in the manifest is the counterexample.

**5. Revision 5 (2026-09-07).** `seams.toml` and `oracle.toml` were written before day 1 (design ledger 9, `data/pre-day-1.md`), which is the acceptance test §12 item 13 named, and it failed twice. The example in §4.1 was not TOML: `name = "x"; budget = 1` on one line does not parse, since TOML has no statement separator, so a row is one key per line and the example is now an excerpt of the file itself. And design §10 states four rules the example had no predicate for: the denied clippy lints (`deny_lints`), a named `xtask` lint such as the re-entry signature rule (`check`), the glossary as a sensor (`avoid_from`), and the generated, committed `ext/clippy.toml` (`generated`). The grammar sentence lists every predicate. D.3 and D.16 are answered by measurement: the `expect` fence's `=` trips `rustdoc::invalid_codeblock_attributes` and the crates that carry witnesses allow it; the PreToolUse and Stop hook shapes, the deny output and the Stop block were exercised end to end on Claude Code 2.1.263 and are recorded in §13. Check: `tomllib` parses the manifest; every `[[rule]]` carries exactly one of the predicates the grammar sentence lists; `permit` on the recorded stdin shapes produces the recorded stdout shapes.

**6. Revision 6 (2026-09-07).** The context layer was built (#7) and three sentences of §5 did not survive contact with the tools. The local file's regions are `volatile`, not `inputs=../target/harness/**`, because `computed` refuses a glob that matches nothing and a fresh clone has no store; `ext/AGENTS.md` lists its extensions with the `tree` loader, because `ls -d */` fails while `ext/` is empty; the `design-of-record` region's inputs are the three design globs, because `*.md` takes in the map itself and its closer sums. Two things §5 left unsaid are now said: `status --session` lays the local file down from its template when the clone has none, and the session hook goes through `cargo run`. The tracked map is rendered against an empty store, every row "no reading", since the readings are one machine's and a rendering of them would churn on every clone; the live numbers are the local file's. Check: `cargo test -p xtask --test map` holds the map under 100 lines with the regions this section names; a second `computed run` after the first writes nothing.

**8. Revision 8 (2026-09-08).** The sensor telemetry was built (#11) and two facts moved into the manifest: `[telemetry] silent_days`, so the fourteen-day window of §7 is the file's and not a constant's, and `[[week]]`, whose reads must name an instrument `xtask` has, so a bar row arrives with its instrument and never before. The reading gained `session`, the hook's `session_id`, and §4.6's "this session" is a rendering of it. The function hook of §4.4 exists as `harness-plugins/`, loaded with `--plugin-dir`; it carries the map through `prompt.context`, and the SessionStart command hook now renders the local file and prints nothing, so a session without the plugin has no map (§5). The check: `cargo test -p xtask` holds the grammar and the renderings; `claude plugin validate harness-plugins --strict` and `tsc -p harness-plugins` hold the adapter.

**9. Revision 9 (2026-09-08).** Two plugins carried one harness: the command hooks in `.claude/skills/third-engine-harness/`, loaded by the skills-directory scan, and the function-hook module in `harness-plugins/`, loaded from the marketplace. They are one plugin now, `harness-plugins/`, whose `hooks.json` carries the three command hooks and names the module. What kept the command hooks a person's was `harness.self` on `.claude/**`; the rule now names `harness-plugins/hooks/hooks.json` and `plugin.json` too, and the `harness` budget counts both trees. Check: `cargo test -p xtask --test map --test done` reads the hooks at the new path; `xtask permit` denies a Write to the plugin's `hooks.json`.

**10. Revision 10 (2026-09-08).** The function hook judges the call (#12). §4.4 had it calling `permit --json`, gated by an environment variable, in about fifteen lines, and the module did not call `permit` at all, since a second judge recorded every note twice. Three things changed: the adapter passes the event on stdin, which answers D.17; `permit --above-the-command-hook` records a denial and leaves a note to the command hook beneath, so the two adapters over one call write one reading each; and the switch is `functionHook` in the plugin's `userConfig`, default true, since revision 8 made the module the map's only path and a session without it has no map. Check: `cargo test -p xtask --test permit` holds the flag and the large write; a headless session with the plugin loaded is refused a `Write` to `seams.toml` by the module, the debug log naming the hooks module, with one `permit` denial in the store for that session.

**11. Revision 11 (2026-09-08).** A `target/release/xtask` older than the manifest it read exited non-zero on every call, and both adapters of §4.4 took the first built binary that existed without asking whether it judged, so `PreToolUse` reported a non-blocking error and every `Edit`, `Write` and `Bash` passed unjudged until the binary was rebuilt. Both adapters now walk their candidates and take the first that exits 0, `permit`'s exit being 0 whatever it decides; the command hook buffers the hook's stdin, since it may hand it to more than one, and its timeout covers the rebuild the last candidate may do. §13 item 8 said the command hook does not fail open: it does, when no candidate judges at all, and the twin rule of §6 is what carried the constraint through the outage, which is the whole of why the twin is required. Two facts §4.4 and §5 still stated were older than revision 9: the adapters' choice of binary, and the plugin loading with `--plugin-dir`. Check: `cargo test -p xtask --test permit` runs the command out of `hooks.json` against a stale build, a stale pair and one that stands, and holds the timeout at 60 or more; run against the command as it stood before, the fallthrough assertion fails and stdout is empty, which is the shape the outage had.

**12. Revision 12 (2026-09-08).** `phpt` landed (#15) and three facts this document stated were wrong or in the wrong place. §4.3 said the oracle module's interface is two functions; running the engine under the recipe and judging an expectation that is a pattern are two more, `run_under` and `verdict`, and the trim set the runner needed was inside `Scratch`, so `normalise` is now its own. The pin's `[recipe.env]` is the witness regime's: `TEST_PHP_EXECUTABLE` names the pinned oracle there, and a phpt lane sets it and its escaped twin to the binary under test, which is what the corpus spawns; the pin does not move, since its value is right for the regime it states. And §5's ceiling counted the whole map, which the map cannot control: the `instruments` region grows one line per instrument and `layout` one per crate, and the phpt row alone took the file to 151. The ceiling is 100 lines outside the computed regions, which is what a person writes and the figure the design wanted before the regions crowded it; the map's prose is 61. What the revision leaves open is §7's per-commit row: the four groups it names are readable by a person and by no command, so the instrument refuses to invent the list and the manifest is where the row belongs; §12 item 17 records the four tests the runner's own control disagrees with the oracle about, whose rows a person writes. Check: `cargo test -p xtask --test map` holds the prose at 100 and fails when a hand-written line is added past it, and passes unchanged when a region grows; `cargo test -p xtask --test oracle` holds `verdict` against `compare` on the same bytes.

**13. Revision 13 (2026-09-09).** The outage of revision 11 was the loud instance of a class whose quiet instances remained (#16): the fallback it added keys on an exit code, and an exit code sees only a build that cannot read the manifest in front of it. A build stale in a way that still parses, a rule removed or a pattern loosened, exits 0 and judges by rules that have moved; a build stale in its own code judges with a matcher since fixed, no rule moved at all. So a build now records what it judges by. `build.rs` writes the digest of `seams.toml` and of `xtask/src` into the binary, `built` compares each with the tree, and `permit` refuses to decide when either differs, which turns the quiet staleness into the loud kind the fallback already walks and closes the ticket's item 3 with it: nothing has to notice before the fact, because the build notices as it is asked. Four more things moved with it. The refusal is a reading, and `status --section permits` carries a row for the two rules `permit` judges by that are its own, so an outage is visible where the facts are, which it was not: through the hour of the outage the store went quiet and the section said the judge was up. `permit` reads the manifest itself, since a manifest it cannot read is a refusal it must record before it exits. `PreToolUse` is two entries: on `Edit` and `Write` a list that no candidate judged is a deny, since a write no judge saw does not proceed, and on `Bash` the call is handed on, since an agent repairs a broken harness with `Bash` and denying all three deadlocks the repair. And the two adapters walk the built binaries in orders that are not equal, the command hook release first and the module debug first, so one stale build no longer silences both layers at once. What the revision leaves open is the rest of the ticket's item 5, a `Bash` call that no judge saw and still writes a file, whose twin is pre-commit. Check: `cargo test -p xtask --test permit` refuses a build over a manifest it was not compiled against and over sources that have moved, records the refusal, holds the two entries to one tool each and their orders to different ones, and denies the write when no candidate judges; run against the command as it stood before, that last assertion sees exit 1 and an empty stdout, which is the shape the hole had.

**14. Revision 14 (2026-09-09).** `done`'s default base was the merge base with `main` and its callers pass nothing, so a turn in a worktree on a track's branch was judged since the track's fork point from `main`: every crate anyone on the track had touched wanted a fresh reading, and an open divergence row anywhere on the track held the turn (#19). The default is the branch's track now, the longest existing branch that is a proper path prefix of its name, and `main` for a branch on none, so a hook that passes nothing is judged against what its worktree is working above. Two things the revision found. The track is the remote's branch and not the clone's own, because git's ref store refuses `refs/heads/t-opus-5` beside `refs/heads/t-opus-5/witness`, one path being a file and a directory at once; a repository whose worktrees are on a track's branches therefore has the track from `origin`, and the ticket's two acceptance lines for the local case and the remote one are one case. And a caller that knows a base the branch cannot say passes it, which pre-push already did: CI's per-commit job triggers on the track branches as well as `main`, and on a pull request it passes the ref the request targets, since its checkout is a detached merge commit that is on no branch. Check: `cargo test -p xtask --test done` takes the track's tip on `t-opus-5/witness` and names only the crate that branch changed, `main` on the track itself, on `main`, on a detached `HEAD` and on a branch whose prefix names no branch, and holds the workflow to passing the pull request's base ref and a push nothing.

**15. Revision 15 (2026-09-09).** A track's worktree was laid down with `git worktree add`, which leaves three of its facts to be discovered by the agent that arrives in it (#20). `computed` trust is keyed by the root path, so a new worktree is untrusted and its regions, its session hook and its first commit all answer with that. `PreToolUse` denies an `Edit` or a `Write` that no candidate judged (revision 13), and in a worktree with a cold `target/` the only candidate is `cargo run … permit` inside the hook's 60 s timeout, so the first write could be denied while the build that would judge it was still going. And `core.hooksPath` and `.claude/settings.json` carry over on their own, being repository config and a tracked file, so a command that sets them again cannot tell a clone that has them from one that does not. §5 names the two commands that make those three measured rather than guessed, `scripts/worktree-add.sh` and `scripts/worktree-retire.sh`, and the second is where a track keeps its evidence: a worktree's readings live in its own `target/harness/`, so its log is appended into the track's store before the worktree that holds it is removed. Check: `cargo test -p xtask --test worktree` runs both halves against a fixture repository whose `xtask` is a stand-in — the branch is taken off `origin/t-x`, and off `main` for a branch on no track; the built binary and the local file are there when the command returns; the trust line is printed as a person's; a second add on the branch or the directory refuses and changes nothing; and after a retire the fixture's own `status --section permits` counts the retired agent's denial.

**16. Revision 16 (2026-09-10).** An architecture review of revision 15's day found the instruments deep and what they share shallow, and sixteen tickets (#40 to #55) deepened the shared ground; this entry records what moved and the decisions taken with it. Five things moved. The track is a module of `xtask`: `xtask track` answers which track a branch is on, the base `done` measures since and the root that holds the track's store, and `xtask worktree add` and `retire` are the two halves #20 wrote in shell, laid at `.claude/worktrees/<branch>` where `permit` already resolves a workspace; `xtask regions` is the one reader of what `computed run` answered. The oracle module owns the runner, the php-src tree's location (`Source::tree`) and the expectation grammar, asserts the pin's digest once per run through `Pin::oracle()`, and one tally behind the phpt and witness lanes owns the runner ladder, the print loop and the rule that nothing run is not green. What is code is decided once in the `rust` module: the code lines `budget` counts, the `unsafe` sites both sensors of `unsafe.homes` find (an `#[unsafe(...)]` attribute is a site), and the `.rs` files the build digests. A reading takes its tree stamp through the manifest's scope mapping and reaches the store through one writer, whose exit rule is one function. And `xtask` has a lock of its own, `xtask/xtask.lock`, its public items being the instruments' run functions, the data modules and the reading. Decisions taken with it: the harness's subcommands that act or answer and write no reading are commands, not instruments (glossary); the shared module is `tally`, and `lane` stays the word for a scheduled run of a corpus; a witness tally with nothing run under an absent oracle is a skip, stated, not a pass (#60); `permit --bench` is a measurement and exits 0 whatever its bar says (#61); a line inside a multi-line string is a counted line, which §4.1's "non-blank, non-comment" already said and the old scanner did not do; a fixture that carries its own manifest is judged by that manifest, not by an `exempt` list (#57); the verb for what an instrument does to a rule or a crate is `hold` (#58), and the harness's rustdoc is to be held to the glossary's Harness section alone: naming `xtask` in `vocab.avoid`'s scope as it stands fired 546 times on the harness's own words (`lock`, `hook`, `region`, `registry`, `run`), which the engine's entries avoid and the harness means, so the scope waits on a rule that reads one section (#63); the Claude Code pin the test job installs stays a pin, and making it a sensed fact is postponed (#62). What stands from earlier entries as records: ledger 13's digest is now of the `.rs` files under `xtask/src` alone, and ledger 15's stand-in `xtask` is gone, the worktree test running against the harness itself. Check: `cargo test -p xtask` is 320 green; `xtask api-check` holds `xtask/xtask.lock`; `xtask budget` reads the harness at about 10.0k of 14k; a call into a fixture with its own `seams.toml` is judged by it once #57 lands, and until then `permit --diff` refuses the fixture's own sources, which is the counterexample the ticket exists for.

**17. Revision 17 (2026-09-11).** Three tickets made the corpus findable on a machine that is not the maintainer's, and this document said the tree was where the pin's path put it. `Source::tree()` reads `TEST_PHP_SRCDIR` first and the pin's `[source].path` second (#36); the test job checks php-src out sparse and shallow at the pin's tag, cached by the pinned commit, and refuses a tree at another commit (#37); a test that would skip for the tree or the binary fails where `XTASK_PROMISED` promised it (#38). §4.3 states the two answers and the promise, §5 says what a machine that is not the maintainer's sets, and §7 says what CI's corpus covers now, with the phpt rows left as the plan. Two decisions: `[source].path` stays in the pin as the second answer, being where the tree is on the machine the pin was written on and nothing a runner reads; and whether the pin names a php the runner can have is D.20, open, with what would settle it. Check: `cargo test -p xtask --test oracle` holds the three cases of `Source::tree()` and the promise's cases; the test job's step "the tree is at the pin's commit" is red on a tree one commit off the pin, read once by hand on a copy of the clone (#37's close); `computed check` holds the region files, the map's `design-of-record` region re-rendered because this file is one of its inputs and its body unchanged.

**18. Revision 18 (2026-09-11).** An unattended run over a frontier of tickets lays a worktree down per ticket, and each waited on a person's `computed trust`, keyed by the worktree's own root since a worktree's `.git` is a file (#74). The decision is a change to the tool and none to the harness: a grant covers every repository whose canonical root has the granted root as a proper path prefix, so the grant on a clone covers its worktrees. §5 and §11 say so; `worktree add` prints no trust line. Rejected: a batch of trust lines before the run, which a frontier that changes as tickets close outgrows, and `--trust` passed from the hooks, which moves a person's judgement into the harness.

**19. Revision 19 (2026-09-11).** `track work` runs a track's frontier from start to retire without a person (#77, #78), and this document never said where the frontier comes from: the run asks `track next` for a ticket, and an unattended track therefore ran until the tickets a person had filed ran out. The plan is the tracker's, and §5 says so. A track's map is one issue, charted by `/frontier chart <days>` at the row of design §11 the engine stands at, its open tickets its sub-issues and its body the route walked and the fog ahead; `/frontier work` files the next batch when the frontier empties, indexing the closes since the last batch and graduating the fog they made sharp. A ticket is a build, a fact outside the tree, or a question the design of record does not make, and the label saying which chooses the session's skill, `/implement`, `/research` or `/grill-design` (#81). §11 says who answers the third kind: on a track nobody sits at the table, so the design of record answers, through `/grill-design`, and a question it is silent on is a person's, filed `for-a-person` and wired to block the tickets waiting on it. An autonomous track therefore stalls on a person and never decides for one, which is the edge of autonomy this harness draws. Rejected: a resident planning agent, one session holding the plan and handing work out. The plan is on the tracker and the evidence in the store, so any session on any model picks the loop up where the last one stopped; a planner that remembers is a planner that has to be the same one next time, and two tracks compared on their models are not comparable through it. Check: `cargo test -p xtask --test track_work` starts one `/frontier` session at an empty frontier on a clone recording `track.frontier=auto` and asks `next` again, and refuses a ticket carrying both `frontier:` labels before any worktree is laid down; a decision ticket the sources answer is closed with the citation on it, and one they are silent on ends open with a `for-a-person` ticket blocking it.

**20. Revision 20 (2026-09-11).** The `harness` row of `[budget]` was written before the repository carried a skill, and §12 item 6 described it as `.claude/**` at a number. Both had fallen behind what the row measures: it counts `harness-plugins/**` as well, and the track's lead now writes skills under `.claude/skills/**`, `/stage-commit`, `/grill-design` and `/frontier`, which a session reads exactly as it reads the map. The ceiling is raised in `seams.toml` and item 6 now says what the row holds, with the number left to the manifest (§14), where a design document's numbers belong. Rejected: scoping `.claude/skills/**` out of the count, which takes the bar off the one tree that grows with every skill the lead writes and opens the item again under a row that still reads green. Check: `cargo xtask budget` passes with the three skills in place and fails at the ceiling the row carried before them (#89, #90).

**21. Revision 21 (2026-09-11).** `track sync` landed with revision 19's run (#80) and §5 never named it: the section described `worktree add`, `worktree retire` and `track work`, and said nothing of the track itself moving, so a reader of §5 alone would take a track to lay its worktrees down off whatever commit it was cut at. The sentence is in §5 now, with the thing that matters when it goes wrong: the merge is made without the working tree, so a conflict refuses and moves nothing rather than leaving a merge in progress for a headless session to sit in. Check: `cargo test -p xtask --test track_sync` (#92).

**22. **23. Revision 23 (2026-09-12).** §4.3 enumerated what an `Invocation` carries as the five things a `.phpt` section adds, and the type had grown twice since that sentence was written on 2026-09-08: `regime` the day after, when the module took the pin's regimes, and `flags` with the compile-identity lane (#101, #113), the flag that stands before the program. **24. Revision 24 (2026-09-12).** Twelve tickets of `t-opus-5` were relabelled `ready-for-agent` and not one of them became workable. Every body named its own act a person's, and three carried an acceptance criterion no label can tick, in as many words: *A person decides between the throwaway manifest row and naming a real extension*. `ticket read` holds a ticket to its boxes verbatim, so such a ticket closes for nobody however finished its code is; #128 had found the shape for one criterion and read it as that ticket's own. Two sentences change. A revision of the design of record is a decision and a wording, and only the first is a person's: where a ticket carries the sentence a person settled, an agent lands it with the ledger row and names the ticket in the commit, which is what #111 and #135 already carry verbatim and could not apply. And a ticket's acceptance criteria are boxes its own label can tick, a decision the work waits on being a `for-a-person` ticket of its own wired to block it, the shape §11 already gives a question the sources are silent on. `AGENTS.md` and `CONTEXT.md`'s Ticket entry say the same. Rejected: a rule denying the three design documents in `permit`, which makes the stall measurable and unblocks nothing; and a grant held for a session that turns a deny off, which is a rule deleted where no diff shows it. **25. Revision 25 (2026-09-12).** §4.5's condition 1 said what a fresh passing reading is and not what a skip is, and `done` answered for two of them in code that this document never stated. #106 settled the witness half and #132 the phpt half, the second after #100 laid twenty `ext/*` crates down with every other instrument green and failed `done` on twenty phpt skips no work in its scope could clear. The condition now says both. A skip the missing engine binary causes is met, and the phpt one is held to its cause: `engine_absent` is how the lane says the binary is what stopped it and not that the subset was empty, which it alone can tell from a subset of a crate not yet created and from one whose every test skipped, with `failed`, `diverged` and `borked` zero beside it so that nothing it did read counts against the crate. It is not held to numbers, nothing having run at all, and a green line for a corpus that never executed would be a claim. The cost stands in the condition rather than hidden: a crate is done with its corpus unrun. The witness half has no rung: it had one of the same shape while the missing binary was why its reading was a skip, held to its numbers, something run and nothing failed or missing (#106), and #119 gave the instrument the linked engine, so a snippet is served in this process and the reading is a pass or a fail on its own merits. Rejected: building `crates/php` and `php_sapi` far enough to run the corpus before the ports that need it, which leaves every crate owning a subset unmeetable meanwhile and is the state that stopped the ticket; serving a `.phpt` in process the way `witness` serves a snippet, which rewrites what the lane is and wants an edge `seams.toml` does not give `xtask`; and leaving the exception in `done`'s rustdoc alone, where a reader of §4.5 would not meet it. Check: `cargo xtask done` is green on a checkout with no engine binary for a crate that owns a subset and is otherwise green, and red for a phpt skip from any other cause; `no_binary_yet` in `xtask/src/done.rs` is the rung, `cargo test -p xtask --test done` runs `the_phpt_skip_the_missing_engine_binary_causes_is_met_and_the_others_are_not`, and the fixtures under `xtask/fixtures/done/` are the counterexamples.

**26. The ticket put three ways to a person — widen the lane, narrow what `done` asks, or take the four out of `php_bundle`'s `default` — and was filed `for-a-person` and relabelled `ready-for-agent` the same day, the hand-over §11's first line asks for, with no one of the three named. At the pinned tag that set is the sixteen, `mbstring`, `pdo_mysql`, `openssl` and `curl` each being an `--enable` or `--with` with no default in its `config.m4`, which is exactly the four rows. So the lane is right and condition 1 is what moves: a crate outside the default build is not asked here, and §7 says in one place what that build is and why the bundle's `default` still reads wider. Rejected: widening the lane, which would run per commit what the design schedules nightly, and leaving the ask where it was, which is the four calls by name every port pays. Not taken here, being a person's file by its own comment: the bundle's `default`, filed as #159. Check: `cargo test -p xtask --test done` and `done`'s `an_optional_crate_is_not_asked_for_a_phpt_reading_the_lane_never_writes`; a bare `cargo xtask phpt` followed by `cargo xtask done` over a base that touches all twenty `ext/` crates printing a phpt condition unmet is this paragraph wrong.

**27. So a criterion written in that shape, #107's fourth, "a witness of `echo` in a loop is measured under the protocol, and the reading is a person's to sign off", had no agent half at all, and the ticket carrying it could not close however finished its code was (#128). The agent runs the protocol's paired reps against the A/A control and writes the reading, scoped by the gate id or the bar's name, exit 0 whatever the reading says; a person reads the number and signs the claim; and a criterion that wants one is two boxes and never one that is both. This is revision 24's rule applied to the one instrument whose reading was always going to be shared. It specifies `bench` rather than building it: no subcommand of `xtask` is `bench` yet, so a criterion of this shape still waits on one, and a ticket carrying such a box splits, the half that names `bench` waiting on the instrument. #107's did, into a read path a witness holds and a number a person signs (#156), and `bench` itself is filed (#155). **33. Revision 33 (2026-09-13).** `xtask steer` landed as the sanctioned writer of the harness's own three files and §11 never named it: Human-only read "Every write to `seams.toml` and `docs/divergence.toml`", which reserved the typing along with the decision, and Agent-default offered the commit-message proposal and nothing else. So a row the maintainer had already ruled on a ticket waited on the same person who had ruled it, which is the stall revision 24 took out of a design revision, here reaching the three files that sentence had not reached. §11 says what is reserved as what it is. What these files *say* is a person's, and so is the hand on any change to something one of them already carries — one changed ceiling, one widened edge, one dropped rule, one reworded reason — that being the half of a write which can lower the bar the work is measured against; `permit` denies the agent the `Edit` and the `Write` on all three either way. What moves is the addition: a settled row reaches the file through `cargo xtask steer`, which an agent runs and which writes only where every table, key, value and array-of-tables row the tracked file carries stands unchanged in the proposal. The repository holds one of each. #152 is the addition, the `[regime.dump]` block settled on the ticket, labelled `ready-for-agent` and landed by an agent; #160 is the change, `xtask`'s ceiling from 14000 to 18000 by a person's hand, which `steer` refuses by construction. §11 also says where that judgement lives, at the write and not in the label, so that #153's reading is not moved back: a criterion naming one of the three is a note naming the instrument, a criterion naming a denied path no instrument writes still fails. Rejected: narrowing #153's exemption to a criterion that names an addition, right in substance and wrong in place, since the criterion's prose cannot tell an addition from a change and `steer` can, exactly, and a heuristic guessing at it would refuse tickets an agent can in fact work, which is the failure #153 exists to stop; and leaving the denial as the whole statement, which reads `permit`'s refusal as the rule rather than as the sensor over it, and keeps every settled row waiting on the person who settled it. Check: `cargo test -p xtask --test steer` runs `a_proposal_that_only_adds_is_written` and `a_changed_recipe_is_refused_and_the_file_stands`, with `every_steerable_file_is_one_permit_denies` holding the two lists to each other, and `cargo test -p xtask --test ticket` runs `a_criterion_whose_object_is_a_file_xtask_steer_writes_is_said_and_not_refused` beside `a_ready_for_agent_ticket_asking_for_a_write_the_manifest_denies_fails_naming_path_and_rule`; a write into one of the three by any hand but a person's or `steer`'s, or a `ready-for-agent` ticket failing on a criterion whose row `steer` could land, is this paragraph wrong (#172).

Revision 34 (2026-09-13).** §4.2's `instrument =` line and `CONTEXT.md`'s **Instrument** entry both named the same fourteen, and the harness had grown five more that neither named: `clippy`, `divergence-reason`, `steer`, `ticket` and `track-work` (#190). `clippy` is the one that cost something, since §4.5 condition 1 asks every touched crate for a fresh `clippy` reading and `done` reads it on every turn, so the instrument the definition of done leans on hardest stood outside the list that says what an instrument is; this is revision 31's shape, a list written before its candidate being silence and not a decision, over five names rather than one. Both lists now name the fifteen subcommands that write a reading, in the order `cli::Command` has them, measured off the code and not off the ticket: every `INSTRUMENT` constant and every literal handed to `Reading::for_crate`, `for_scope` and `stamped_over` under `xtask/src/`, less the three that are a test's own and the one `status` folds for a rendering. `bench`, `crosscheck`, `fuzz` and `mutants` stay in the lists, marked as named and not built, which is what a design of record is for. The doc comment on `Reading::instrument`, a third copy that had drifted its own way to sixteen names, says the same thing and cites this section. `CONTEXT.md`'s **Command** entry keeps `track`, with the clause that `track work` is the one subcommand of a command that is an instrument: what decides which of the two a subcommand is, is the reading and never the name it hangs under, and `track` bare, `new`, `next` and `sync` write none. Rejected: dropping `track` from the **Command** entry, the criterion's other branch, which would say `xtask track` writes a reading when it does not and would leave three commands of the four unnamed; and putting the built and the unbuilt names in one undifferentiated run, which is the state that let five names go missing, since a reader cannot tell a name waiting to be built from a name that should have been added. Check: for every subcommand of `xtask`, the instrument of the readings it records is a name the two lists carry, and every built name in the lists is a subcommand that records one — `grep -rn "INSTRUMENT\|Reading::for_crate\|Reading::for_scope\|Reading::stamped_over" xtask/src/` against the lists is the measurement, and a name in the code and in neither list, or a built name in a list that no subcommand writes, is this paragraph wrong (#190).

**35. Revision 35 (2026-09-13).** `xtask bench` writes a reading (#155) and `status --section bench` renders it, and §5's list of local sections was silent on whether it is a ninth, the shape revision 31 named: a list written before its candidate. Decided on the ticket, and the answer is no (#193). The eight are what something holds a session to — `budget`, `phpt`, `witness`, `mutants`, `divergence` and `permits` are read by `done` or by a rule, `git` is where the session stands, and `port-map` is the row of §11 the track is measured against — and each is a fact about the tree the session is about to change. A `bench` reading holds a session to nothing: what it means is a person's sign-off beside its A/A control (§4.2, §11), and §4.3 says about one row in seventeen resolves on a null effect, so a number rendered in front of every session is a number that will be cited without its control, the one failure the sign-off exists to prevent. Revision 31's test for `port-map`, volatile in the way the seven are, is not enough on its own: `bench` is volatile too, but in a different way, moving when someone runs it and not when the tree moves. So §5 now carries the general form beside the ruling, which is the test for whoever proposes the next ninth. `AGENTS.md`'s prose, which named seven and had missed `port-map` since #148, names the eight `status::LOCAL_SECTIONS` carries; the constant and the template do not move. Rejected: the `bench` row, the ticket's other branch, which puts a claim in the one file where the convention is that a number is a fact; and leaving §5 at the ruling without the test, which answers this ticket and not the next one. Check: `cargo test -p xtask` holds `status::LOCAL_SECTIONS` at eight with no `bench` among them, and `AGENTS.md`'s one sentence on the local file names the same eight in the constant's order; a ninth section landed without a sentence in §5 saying which side of the test it falls on is this paragraph wrong (#193).

**36. Revision 36 (2026-09-13).** A red `cargo test -p php_engine` reached the track tip twice in one day and the harness said nothing either time (#191): `done` walked seven conditions and none of them ran a crate's own tests, the place a kernel's semantics and a native's arity errors are actually pinned, so the harness measured everything about a crate but whether it works, and both were found by the next session to run `cargo test` by hand. The ruling was the orchestrating session's, delegated by the maintainer on 2026-09-13, and this entry lands it. `test` is an instrument: `cargo xtask test [--crate <name>] [--staged]` runs `cargo test -p <crate>` as the reading `done` reads, one per crate, `passed`, `failed`, `ignored` and `filtered` summed off cargo's own lines in `numbers`, `status` pass or fail, exiting non-zero on a failure the way `clippy` does; the child's environment has git's hook variables scrubbed, since pre-commit runs it and a harness test that lays a scratch repository down under an exported `GIT_DIR` would write into the repository being committed to. §4.5 condition 1 asks it of every touched crate, narrowed for nobody, and says why a reading and not a line in the pre-commit script: the stamp. §4.2's `instrument =` line, `CONTEXT.md`'s **Instrument** entry and `Reading::instrument`'s comment name it, revision 34's three lists moving together; §4.6 has the `test` section and §6 and §7 the wiring, pre-commit on the touched crates and CI on the workspace, which runs the harness's own tests a second time after the test job's since that job promises the corpus and this one writes the reading. §5's local file does not carry the reading, decided rather than left silent: `done` names the crate whose test reading is missing, stale or red at every turn's end, so a session reads it there, and the local file's eight are what a session reads before it touches anything. Rejected: a bare `cargo test` line in the pre-commit script, which has no stamp; and narrowing `test` for the harness the way `witness` is, on the cost of its suite, which would leave `xtask`, the one crate whose tests hold every other reading honest, the one crate not held to its own. Check: `cargo test -p xtask --test test` runs `a_crate_with_a_failing_test_reads_fail_with_the_passed_and_the_failed_counted` and `done_is_red_for_a_crate_with_a_failing_test_and_green_once_it_passes` over `xtask/fixtures/test/failing`, one crate with two tests of which one fails, and `cargo test -p xtask --test done` holds `done::FRESH` at eight with `test` asked of `xtask`; a touched crate with a red `cargo test -p` and a green `done` is this paragraph wrong.

**37. Revision 37 (2026-09-13).** `track work` recorded what a session came to and nothing of what it spent (#210): the session is `claude -p …` with its streams the caller's, so tokens, cost and turns left with the process, and the one place a token-to-outcome delta existed was a person reading the run's stdout. The one live figure is the plugin's, `$.session.usage()`, the `/cost` ledger and the context window's fill, and `turn.complete` fires once a turn; so the module's third hook pipes what `usage()` answered to `xtask usage record --session <id> --turn <n>`, an instrument that writes one reading of instrument `usage`, `usd`, `tokens`, `window`, `percent` and `turn` in its numbers, scoped to the branch's ticket where one is recorded and to the session id otherwise, so an interactive session on `main` still shows up; the last reading under a scope is the session's final figure by construction. Usage is a reading and not a judgement: the reading passes whatever the numbers say, and a `usage()` that rejects or an `xtask` that does not run is one `$.ui.log` line and the turn proceeds. `track work` owns the join: `hold` reads the last `usage` reading under the ticket's scope, from the standing worktree's own store where one stands and from the track's log otherwise, which is where a retire put it, and puts `usd`, `tokens` and `turn` on the step's `track-work` reading beside the commit; the run's own reading carries the `usd` total. A ticket with no usage reading, a stand-in program in a test, carries none of the three and renders blank, not zero. §4.2's `instrument =` line, `CONTEXT.md`'s **Instrument** entry and `Reading::instrument`'s comment name `usage`, revision 34's three lists moving together; §4.6 has the `usage` section and the `track` section's three columns, and §5 says `usage` is rendered by name and not a ninth local section, by revision 35's test: what a session spent is an answer to a question a session chose to ask, not a fact about the tree it is about to change. Rejected: `--output-format json` on the `claude -p` call in `track work`, which yields the total once the session has ended, gives the same number a second path, and is not live; and a per-turn reading from the plugin joined nowhere, which would leave the figure beside the ticket it was spent on rather than with it. Check: `cargo test -p xtask --lib usage` reads a turn under the branch's ticket and under a session with none, and the join with and without a usage reading; `cargo test -p xtask --test track_work` runs `the_frontier_is_worked_until_it_empties_past_a_ticket_left_open` with stand-in sessions that each record one, and holds the `track` section to the retired ticket's usd read from the track's log, the standing one's read from its own store, and the run's total; a `track-work` reading whose session recorded usage and which carries no `usd` is this paragraph wrong.

**40. Revision 40 (2026-09-14).** Which warm start a worktree gets (#251, #245). #245 asked `worktree add` to point every worktree of a track at one `CARGO_TARGET_DIR` under the clone's `target/`, on the premise that a workspace crate's artifacts are keyed by source hash and do not collide between worktrees at different commits, so the registry dependencies would compile once per track instead of once per worktree, which three worktrees laid down within a minute had put at a load average of 222 on 12 cores. Measured under the pinned toolchain, cargo 1.95.0, the premise does not hold: cargo keys a workspace member's artifact by its path under the workspace root and judges the slot fresh by mtime, with no source-hash freshness on stable (`-Zchecksum-freshness` is nightly's), so two copies of a two-crate workspace on one target directory, the second's sources dated before the first's build, had `cargo run` in the second execute the first's binary with the first's `CARGO_MANIFEST_DIR` baked in, and once the second had built, the first's `cargo build` finished in 0.00 s on the second's binary (E1 and E2 on #251). A worktree laid down off the track tip has every file it did not edit dated at its checkout, so for the harness that is a wrong path and not a slow one: `built_root` resolves `--root` to the workspace the binary was compiled in, `built::judges` compares its digest against that root and `permit` judges an edit with digests compiled from the tree it judges, so `done` run in one worktree would measure another's checkout and write to its store. The ruling was made on the maintainer's behalf in the run working this track's `for-a-person` tickets and this entry lands it: the worktree's `target/` is its own, and `add` seeds it from the clone's `target/debug` with `cp -c`, a clone-on-write copy where the volume supports one, so the dependencies are blocks the two directories share and cargo rebuilds only the workspace's own crates, since a fresh checkout's files are newer than the seeded artifacts; measured at `543e502`, 10.09 s of seeding with no byte copied, then `cargo build -p xtask` in 7.56 s compiling 25 crates, every one a workspace member, against a cold build of minutes. Nothing the harness can observe is shared: no build lock between worktrees, `target/debug/xtask` where the hooks, `scripts/worktree-add.sh`, `worktree::built` and `built_root` look, and `retire` removes the copy with the worktree. Seeding is best-effort, so a `cp -c` that cannot, a volume without clonefile, leaves that worktree cold and nothing else changed. §5 "A track's worktrees" carries the sentence, and #245's "What to build" and criteria are rewritten to it, its tracked `.cargo/config.toml` dropped since no variable is set. Rejected: the shared directory as filed, on more than the hazard, since `built_root`, `built::judges` and `hooks.json`'s walk each rest on a binary that belongs to one checkout and would all be rewritten around one that is any worktree's; and the cold build kept, which is honest and gives up a measured trade of ten seconds against minutes. Check: `cargo test -p xtask --test worktree` holds, once #245 lands, that a worktree laid down has `target/debug/xtask` of its own with `--root` the worktree and no `CARGO_TARGET_DIR` in any `.cargo/config.toml` cargo reads there, and that the build `add` ran compiled no registry dependency; a worktree whose `xtask` names another checkout as its root is this paragraph wrong.

It needs the pending throwable's last owner released while `EG(exception)` still points at it, and php releases one in that state in two places only: `zend_clear_exception` nulls the slot before `OBJ_RELEASE`, under php's own comment `/* exception may have destructor */` (`Z/Zend/zend_exceptions.c:251-254`), so it is not one; `zend_user_exception_handler` releases `EG(exception)` before nulling it (`Z/Zend/zend.c:1949-1953`) but is reached only with `EG(current_execute_data)` null, where `zend_call_function`'s tail renders the handler's own throwable uncaught and `zend_bailout`s first (`Z/Zend/zend_execute_API.c:1069-1076`); and `zend_exception_set_previous` from `zend_throw_exception_internal`, which releases the slot's object before the slot is reassigned (`Z/Zend/zend_exceptions.c:201-202`), fires only where a `previous` chain or the throw itself holds that object a second time. Nine snippets under the pin build the closest states the language can — a handler that throws a destructible throwable, a destructor that rethrows what is pending, a throwable whose chain holds the one being thrown, and one held past its own fatal by a variable, by a static property and by itself — and none prints the text. So the fence is owed to no ticket that exists, and #458 is filed for the question that leaves: whether a copied branch php itself cannot reach stands on a unit test, or comes out. Check: a snippet under the pin that prints `Attempt to destruct pending exception` is this entry wrong and the fence owed that day; until one does, `cargo test -p php_engine --features test` holds the branch by the named test and `cargo xtask witness --crate php_engine` counts it among the kernels it covers.

**58. Revision 58 (2026-09-27).** How `witness.coverage` knows a fence exercised a method (#966). Revision 57 counted a method as covered when a fence of its class spelled a call to it, `->name(` or `::name(`. #953 built that, and its builder found the gap: php calls many methods for you. `__construct` runs through `new C(`, `__toString` through a string conversion, `offsetGet` through `$c[...]`, `getIterator` and `current` through `foreach`, `count` through `count($c)`. A method reached only incidentally, by another class's fence, is not covered by that fence: what counts is what its own class's author set out to prove, and a coverage failure then points at the method's class, not at an edit elsewhere. The recording is a cargo feature, so the `php` binary and the bench carry none of it. Feature unification can turn it on in a workspace build, so it also records nothing until the witness runner switches it on. It records in the entry itself and not at the executor's call site, so every PHP-visible path is seen once and the hot dispatch loop is untouched. A direct Rust-to-Rust call is not seen, and carries no PHP-level proof. Unconverted natives have no such entry, so they are never recorded, and their crates keep the count they had until #948 moves them. Accepted costs: the engine helps measure itself, so a fixture holds the recording; a native reached only from Rust reads `missing`; and coverage needs a run of the linked engine, which `witness` makes anyway. A `with` phrase is the program and its subcommands with only options between them, and a `deny_argv` element is one of three typed shapes: an option among the command's own words before `--`, a short cluster included (`-anm`); an option with the setting it names (`-c core.hooksPath`, the key compared as git compares it); or a subcommand. Every `deny_argv` rule reads the same argv, so `ticket.close` gains the precision too, and the manifest refuses any other element shape. A merge makes a state of the crate no branch measured, so the reading over the tree now cannot carry a verdict a branch's `--accept` captured across it. The sentence that follows reads "neither kind of evidence" where it read "no such reading", which the new sentence would otherwise contradict. Check: a hand-typed `exit=` line is denied on a merged branch, and a captured one is permitted (#1448's fixtures).

**69. Revision 69 (2026-10-03).** The dashboard is a named reader of the harness, and `xtask status` renders a log it is handed. It widens what `status` reads past the manifest, the registry and the store. §4.6 gains the sentence on `--every`, `--log` and the `v` a `--format jsonl` row carries. §4.2's Readers gains the dashboard, which reads through `xtask status --format jsonl --every --log` and names the xtask commit that rendered. `v` versions a row's shape only. A change of rules is dated by that commit and reviewed as a fixture diff, never by a number bumped by hand. What a phpt row carries lives in the phpt section's rustdoc and its fixture (#1745). That the published history keeps each reading whole goes in the dashboard's README (#1747). `CONTEXT.md` gains no entry. Nothing is reversed, so there is no ADR. Revision 75 (2026-10-04).** The frontier's rule and `/frontier`'s arguments, settled by Mitchell on #1843, #1849 and #1839 and landed by #1851. #1755 left a closed ticket's branch for /specs to merge, so a ticket whose blocker closed in the same run was named and cut from a `main` without its blocker's work; Mitchell ruled on #1843 to hold it back until merged, and #1850 built it, `track_next::waits_on` the one statement. #1836 made a ticket with no `track:` label `main`'s alone, and the old clause, "carry no `track:` label naming another track", is met by an unlabelled ticket for every track (#1849). §5's A track's plan and `CONTEXT.md`'s **Frontier** carry the settled clause, the open tickets labelled `ready-for-agent` "that no ticket blocks while it is open or while the branch that records it is not yet in `main`, that are the track's (carry its `track:` label, or, for `main`, no `track:` label at all), and that no branch of the repository records", §5 keeping its own grammar around it. #1750 made `/frontier` take the track as its argument, since a track is a label and nothing reads it from where a session runs (ADR-0009); **Chart** names `/frontier chart <track> <days>` and `/frontier work <track>`, and §11's Human-only list `/frontier chart <track> <days>` (#1839). **76. Revision 76 (2026-10-04).** A field added to a `--format jsonl` row keeps `v` where its absence is already read right, settled by Mitchell on #1834. Mitchell ruled that additive fields keep `v`, and §4.6 carries the sentence after the paragraph on `--every`, `--log` and `v`, word for word as it now reads; the rustdoc of `V` in each `row` module says the same, and `target.missed_skipped` lands under `v = 1`. Nothing is reversed, so there is no ADR. Check: `cargo test -p xtask` runs `phpt::row`'s `a_row_carries_the_skipped_misses_where_the_reading_counts_them`, in which a reading without the count renders its target with no `missed_skipped` field, and `npm test` in `dashboard/` decodes a `v = 1` row with the field and without it. **77. Revision 77 (2026-10-04).** What `status` reads, its sections by `--help`, and `/frontier` with its track, settled by Mitchell on #1852, #1854 and #1856 and landed by #1858. It keeps a sentence only where a section's meaning is not plain from its name, the `rules` sentence #1772 settled and "`locks` is also the `api-check` readings' section (`--section api-check`)", and the synopsis #1840 settled stays. #1750 made `/frontier` take the track as its argument (ADR-0009), and four passages outside the sentences revision 75 landed still named it in its old shapes; as Mitchell settled on #1856, `CONTEXT.md`'s **Frontier** reads "which is when `/frontier work <track>` fills it again", §5's A track's plan `chart <track> <days>` and `work <track>`, and §11's Human-only and Agent-default lines `/frontier work <track>`, each keeping its own grammar. The ledger's own revisions keep their words as a record. Nothing is reversed, so there is no ADR.
